<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that, for every fixed <i>C</i> &gt; 1, approximating Uniform Sparsest Cut within factor <i>C</i> is NP-hard. The output graphs have nonnegative rational capacities and unit demand between every pair of distinct vertices.

- **claim-uniform-sparsest-cut-hardness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every fixed real constant $C>1$, there is a polynomial-time reduction from $3$CNF satisfiability to finite undirected graphs with nonnegative rational capacities and exactly unit demands, together with a positive rational threshold $a$, such that $$\begin{array}{ll} \text{the formula is satisfiable}&\Longrightarrow\ \Phi(G)\le a,\\[2pt] \text{the formula is unsatisfiable}&\Longrightarrow\ \Phi(G)>Ca. \end{array}$$ The graph size and the bit lengths of all output numbers are polynomial in the formula size, with constants and exponents allowed to depend on $C$. In particular, approximating Uniform Sparsest Cut within any fixed factor $C>1$ is NP-hard.
  - Explanation: An unconditional reduction produces a multiplicative gap for Uniform Sparsest Cut with rational capacities and unit demands. The soundness guarantee includes every nonempty proper cut, and the polynomial bounds permit dependence on the fixed approximation factor.
- **claim-quadratic-evaluation-pencil** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-pencil)): For every $0<\lambda_0<1$ and every sufficiently large power of two $r$, put $q=r^2$. There is a deterministic algorithm which, given an integer $d\geq 1$, constructs a list $$u_1,\ldots,u_m\in\mathbb F_q^d$$ with the following properties:* 1. *$m=O(d)$, and the list contains every standard basis vector of $\mathbb F_q^d$;* 2. *if a homogeneous quadratic polynomial $Q$ in $d$ variables does not vanish at every point of the list, then $$\#\{j:Q(u_j)=0\}\leq\lambda_0m.$$* *The running time is $2^{O(d)}$ bit operations. The constants in both bounds may depend on $r$, which is fixed independently of $d$.
  - Explanation: A deterministic construction supplies a list of evaluation vectors containing the standard basis. A homogeneous quadratic that is nonzero somewhere on this list has few zero evaluations; the guarantee is relative to the list rather than to all quadratic polynomials as formal expressions.
- **claim-simple-pole-extension** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-pencil-simple-pole)): Let $b\in k((z))$ have order $-1$. The polynomial $X^r+X-b$ is irreducible over $k((z))$. Its extension is a Laurent series field $k((w))$, where $w=1/X$ and $z$ has order $r$ in $w$. Its integral lattice has the basis $$1,w,\ldots,w^{r-1}\quad\text{over }k[[z]],$$ and the discriminant of this basis has $z$-order $2(r-1)$.
  - Explanation: A simple pole forces irreducibility of the additive extension. The reciprocal of the new generator is a local parameter, giving an integral basis and an explicit discriminant order.
- **claim-bit-test-system** (construction, [source](#stmt-thm-bit-test-system)): For every fixed $\lambda>0$ there are constants $\eta>0$, $B<\infty$, and a fixed finite list of bounded-arity test laws with the following properties. From a Boolean $3$CNF formula one can construct, in polynomial time, a finite uniform space $\mathcal S$ of bit positions and these test laws on $\mathcal S$.* 1. *Every individual question marginal is at most $B$ times uniform measure on $\mathcal S$. All samplers are polynomially enumerable finite weighted lists with rational probabilities; every positive seed probability is at least inverse-polynomial in the input size.* 2. *If the formula is satisfiable, there is an assignment $y:\mathcal S\to\mathbb F_2$ accepted by every test in every support.* 3. *If the formula is unsatisfiable, every assignment fails one of the test laws with probability greater than $\eta$.* 4. *There is also an exchangeable law of $(s_1,s_2,s_3,s_4)\in\mathcal S^4$, with uniform marginals, such that the conditional expectation operator of any coordinate given any other has norm at most $\lambda$ on mean-zero $L^2(\mathcal S)$. The assignment in <span class="upright">(ii)</span> satisfies $$y_{s_1}+y_{s_2}+y_{s_3}+y_{s_4}=0 \qquad\text{in }\mathbb F_2$$ throughout the support of this law.* *All constants, including the degrees of the polynomial size and time bounds, depend only on $\lambda$. Two independent copies of the law in <span class="upright">(iv)</span>, paired coordinatewise, have the same pair-operator bound on $\mathcal S^2$ and give the corresponding parity identity for the expressions $y_i+y_j$.
  - Explanation: The construction transforms a Boolean formula into bounded-arity tests with controlled query marginals, perfect completeness, and a fixed soundness gap. An auxiliary exchangeable parity law has uniform marginals and strongly mixing coordinate pairs.
- **claim-matrix-walk-mixing** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-matrix-walk)): On mean-zero $L^2(G)$, the operator $\mathsf T$ has norm at most $\lambda_1=\lambda_0+q^{-1}$. In particular, for every nonempty $D\subseteq G$, $$\label{eq:internal-edge-bound} \frac{\ip{\mathbf 1_D}{\mathsf T\mathbf 1_D}}{\mu(D)} \leq \mu(D)+\lambda_1.$$
  - Explanation: Additive characters diagonalize the matrix walk. The pencil controls the directions that can give a trivial character average, and the resulting spectral estimate bounds internal transition mass for every nonempty set.
- **claim-masked-pair-mixing** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-masked-pair-mixing)): Every pair-operator of Equation <a href="#eq:four-masked-positions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:four-masked-positions">[eq:four-masked-positions]</a> has mean-zero norm at most $$\max\{\lambda_1,\,2/(q-3)\}.$$
  - Explanation: Each masked pair operator splits into a spatial walk and a mask permutation average. Their centered norms give the stated maximum bound.
- **claim-minor-product-slack** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-minor-product-slack)): A linear combination of products $p_I(T)p_J(T)$ that vanishes on all Boolean diagonal matrices is a sum of terms of the form $$\label{eq:slack-coefficient-patterns} cMN\,T_{ij},\qquad cMN\,T_{ij}(T_{ii}-1),\qquad cMN\,(T_{ii}^2-T_{ii}),$$ where $i\ne j$ in the first two forms, $c\in\mathbb F$, and $M,N$ are arbitrary square minors of $T$, possibly empty.
  - Explanation: Products of principal minors reduce to a canonical principal minor plus explicit residual terms. Vanishing on Boolean diagonals cancels the canonical part, leaving precisely the permitted slack forms.
- **claim-quadratic-line-correction** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-quadratic-correction)): Put $\gamma=10^{-3}$. Suppose the line law of a function $P:G\to\mathbb F$ fails with probability at most $\delta^2$, where $\delta>0$ satisfies $$\begin{aligned} 2\delta+\lambda_1&<\gamma/2,\notag\\ 1-4\gamma-4\delta-2/(q-1)&>\delta+\lambda_1,\label{eq:correction-smallness}\\ 27\gamma+5\delta&<1.\notag \end{aligned}$$ There is a scalar-invariant set $H$ of directions of $\mathsf D$-mass at least $1-\delta$, and a function $P'$ differing from $P$ on at most $2\delta$ of $G$, such that $t\mapsto P'(A+th)$ has degree at most two for every $A$ and $h\in H$.
  - Explanation: A function that rarely fails the line test can be changed on a small set so that all bases obey exact quadratic line identities along a common high-mass set of directions. The proof fixes modal values consistently during point filling.
- **claim-line-support-gap** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-line-support-gap)): Suppose a scalar-invariant set of directions has $\mathsf D$-mass at least $1-\rho$, and $F:G\to\mathbb F$ has degree at most $D<q-1$ on every line in those directions. Then either $F=0$ identically or $$\mu(\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}F)\geq 1-\rho-\frac{D}{q-1}-\lambda_1.$$
  - Explanation: A nonzero function with bounded degree along a high-mass scalar-invariant direction set must occupy a large fraction of the matrix space. The argument combines the root bound on each good line with the matrix-walk internal-edge estimate.
- **claim-calibrated-valuation-obstruction** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-event-obstruction)): There is a fixed finite family of primitive types $(P_s,p_s)$ and ordered comparisons, and a fixed $\zeta>0$, constructible in polynomial time from the input formula, with the following properties.* 1. *Each predicate depends on boundedly many bit positions. All samplers are polynomially enumerable finite rational laws with inverse-polynomial positive seed probabilities.* 2. *Constant-one and constant-zero types are included, with calibrations one and zero. Every comparison marginal is a primitive type, and every type has a comparison to the constant-one type.* 3. *In a satisfiable instance, evaluation at the perfect assignment $y^*$ satisfies $\mathbb E_{P_s}R(y^*)=p_s$ for every type.* 4. *In an unsatisfiable instance, no Boolean valuation is $\zeta$-calibrated and ordered.* *The numbers $p_s$, the number of types and comparisons, and $\zeta$ are independent of the input size.
  - Explanation: A finite primitive predicate system admits the prescribed calibrations at a satisfying assignment. On an unsatisfiable formula, simultaneous approximate calibration and order preservation would decode an assignment contradicting test soundness.
- **claim-rare-event-valuation-extraction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-rare-event)): Fix the numerical type data, the comparison-type pairs, positive constants $b_0,K_0,B_0$, and a tolerance $\zeta>0$. There is an integer $t_0$ such that, for every $t\ge t_0$, there is $\varepsilon_0(t)>0$ with the following property. Set $p=p_*^t$ and take $0<\varepsilon\le\varepsilon_0(t)$. Suppose that $0<v\le B_0$ and that $W$ is a Boolean function of the actual conjunction predicate satisfying $$\begin{aligned} \mathbb P_{P_*^t}(W(r)=1)&\ge b_0vp, \label{eq:entropy-iid-success}\\ \mathbb P_{\boldsymbol s}(W(r)=1) &\le v\bigl(K_0p_{\boldsymbol s}+\varepsilon\bigr) \quad\text{for every type pattern }\boldsymbol s, \label{eq:entropy-pattern-upper}\\ \mathbb P\bigl(W(r)>W(r')\bigr)&\le\varepsilon v \quad\text{for every lifted comparison.} \label{eq:entropy-lifted-order} \end{aligned}$$ Then there exists a single Boolean valuation $H$ satisfying Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-valuation-target" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:entropy-valuation-target">[eq:entropy-valuation-target]</a>.* *The choices of $t_0$ and $\varepsilon_0(t)$ depend only on $b_0,K_0,B_0,\zeta$, the finite type and comparison lists, and $(\omega_s,p_s)_{s\in\mathcal T}$. They are uniform in $\mathcal X$, the primitive supports, their atom probabilities, and the particular comparison couplings with the prescribed marginals. Types with $p_s=0$ are allowed.
  - Explanation: Success of a Boolean response to conjunctions, together with patternwise upper bounds and lifted order bounds, forces a single calibrated and ordered valuation. The constants are uniform over predicate supports and atom probabilities, including types with zero calibration.
- **claim-analytic-score-gap** (construction, [source](#stmt-thm-score-gap)): For each fixed $T_0\ge1$, the primitive system of Section <a href="#sec:events" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:events">4</a> admits a demand law $\mu$ of scores and a finite sum $\mathcal D$ of raw comparison forms with the following properties. All arities, score bounds, numerical dimensions, and total comparison weight are bounded by constants independent of the input size. The laws and weights do not depend on a lookup. At a perfect witness $x$, $$\int\|B(x)-B'(x)\|\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m(B,B')\le4, \qquad \mu\{B:B(x)\ge1/4\},\ \mu\{B:B(x)\le-1/4\}\ge10^{-4}.$$ In a NO instance, every binary measurable lookup with $\nu=\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\mu(h)>0$ satisfies $\mathcal D(h)>T_0\nu$. Conditional on the discrete sampling data, the numerical laws and score entries belong to fixed finite families of polynomials with rational coefficients on rational boxes, with atoms represented as separate cases.
  - Explanation: Demand scores and positive comparison measures separate perfect witnesses from every measurable binary lookup of positive demand variance on an unsatisfiable instance. All numerical dimensions and polynomial patterns are fixed independently of input size.
- **claim-amplitude-signal** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-amplitude-signal)): Choose $k=\lceil\rho^{-1}\rceil$ and require $n_j\ge4k$. With $c=4^{-(k+2)}$, Equation <a href="#eq:resampling-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:resampling-bound">[eq:resampling-bound]</a> implies $$\label{eq:amplitude-signal} \\|b^0\\|_\Sigma^2\le\nu,\qquad \sum_j s_j^2\sum_{i\in I_j}\mathbb E[\|b_i^0\|^2\mid\mathcal B]\le\tfrac14, \qquad \\|g^0\\|_\Sigma^2\ge c\nu.$$ The constants $k,c$ depend only on $T_0$.
  - Explanation: Low disagreement under slot resampling leaves an amplitude-dependent component after random half-freezing. Orthogonal product decompositions bound the total component energy and retain a definite fraction in the conditional projection.
- **claim-finite-score-form-structure** (classification, [source](#stmt-prop-score-compilation)): The demand law and the raw comparison measures in Theorem <a href="#thm:score-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:score-gap">13</a> have polynomially enumerable discrete seeds, whose positive probabilities are bounded below by an inverse polynomial in input size. For each seed, after finitely many fixed subdivisions of numerical boxes, their conditional densities are nonnegative rational polynomials and the score table entries are rational polynomials of bounded degree in a fixed number of numerical variables. There are only finitely many such numerical polynomial patterns, independent of input size. The total raw comparison mass is finite and bounded by a constant. For any fixed finite grid and deterministic entrywise rounding rule, every positive rounded demand mass or comparison-pair mass has an inverse-polynomial lower bound.
  - Explanation: The continuous experiments have enumerable discrete seeds and a fixed finite collection of rational polynomial numerical patterns. After fixed rounding, every positive demand or comparison mass retains an inverse-polynomial lower bound.
- **claim-rational-capacity-demand-compilation** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-lem-rational-compilation)): In polynomial time one can compute nonnegative rational numbers $c_{ij}$ and $l_i$ such that $$\begin{aligned} &c_{ij}\ge c^0_{ij},\qquad \sum_{i<j}(c_{ij}-c^0_{ij})\le1,\label{eq:capacity-upper}\\ &0\le l_i\le\mu_i,\qquad L:=\sum_i l_i\ge1-10^{-6}. \label{eq:demand-lower} \end{aligned}$$ Their binary encoding lengths are polynomial. Every positive $l_i$ is at least an inverse polynomial in the input size, with the polynomial depending only on $C$.
  - Explanation: Rational box subdivision produces upper capacities and lower demand masses with controlled aggregate errors. Positive retained masses remain sufficiently large for polynomial-size replication, and all rational encodings have polynomial length.

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-event-obstruction",
        "exact_quote": "There is a fixed finite family of primitive types $(P_s,p_s)$ and ordered comparisons, and a fixed $\\zeta>0$, constructible in polynomial time from the input formula, with the following properties.*\n\n1.  *Each predicate depends on boundedly many bit positions. All samplers are polynomially enumerable finite rational laws with inverse-polynomial positive seed probabilities.*\n\n2.  *Constant-one and constant-zero types are included, with calibrations one and zero. Every comparison marginal is a primitive type, and every type has a comparison to the constant-one type.*\n\n3.  *In a satisfiable instance, evaluation at the perfect assignment $y^*$ satisfies $\\mathbb E_{P_s}R(y^*)=p_s$ for every type.*\n\n4.  *In an unsatisfiable instance, no Boolean valuation is $\\zeta$-calibrated and ordered.*\n\n*The numbers $p_s$, the number of types and comparisons, and $\\zeta$ are independent of the input size.",
        "source_id": "PM-117.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-event-obstruction",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-rare-event",
        "exact_quote": "Fix the numerical type data, the comparison-type pairs, positive constants $b_0,K_0,B_0$, and a tolerance $\\zeta>0$. There is an integer $t_0$ such that, for every $t\\ge t_0$, there is $\\varepsilon_0(t)>0$ with the following property. Set $p=p_*^t$ and take $0<\\varepsilon\\le\\varepsilon_0(t)$. Suppose that $0<v\\le B_0$ and that $W$ is a Boolean function of the actual conjunction predicate satisfying $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\mathbb P_{P_*^t}(W(r)=1)&\\ge b_0vp,\n \\label{eq:entropy-iid-success}\\\\\n \\mathbb P_{\\boldsymbol s}(W(r)=1)\n &\\le v\\bigl(K_0p_{\\boldsymbol s}+\\varepsilon\\bigr)\n \\quad\\text{for every type pattern }\\boldsymbol s,\n \\label{eq:entropy-pattern-upper}\\\\\n \\mathbb P\\bigl(W(r)>W(r')\\bigr)&\\le\\varepsilon v\n \\quad\\text{for every lifted comparison.}\n \\label{eq:entropy-lifted-order}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Then there exists a single Boolean valuation $H$ satisfying Equation <a href=\"#eq:entropy-valuation-target\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"eq:entropy-valuation-target\">[eq:entropy-valuation-target]</a>.*\n\n*The choices of $t_0$ and $\\varepsilon_0(t)$ depend only on $b_0,K_0,B_0,\\zeta$, the finite type and comparison lists, and $(\\omega_s,p_s)_{s\\in\\mathcal T}$. They are uniform in $\\mathcal X$, the primitive supports, their atom probabilities, and the particular comparison couplings with the prescribed marginals. Types with $p_s=0$ are allowed.",

      },
      "evidence": [
        {

Suppose that $0<v\\le B_0$ and that $W$ is a Boolean function of the actual conjunction predicate satisfying $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\mathbb P_{P_*^t}(W(r)=1)&\\ge b_0vp,\n \\label{eq:entropy-iid-success}\\\\\n \\mathbb P_{\\boldsymbol s}(W(r)=1)\n &\\le v\\bigl(K_0p_{\\boldsymbol s}+\\varepsilon\\bigr)\n \\quad\\text{for every type pattern }\\boldsymbol s,\n \\label{eq:entropy-pattern-upper}\\\\\n \\mathbb P\\bigl(W(r)>W(r')\\bigr)&\\le\\varepsilon v\n \\quad\\text{for every lifted comparison.}\n \\label{eq:entropy-lifted-order}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Then there exists a single Boolean valuation $H$ satisfying Equation <a href=\"#eq:entropy-valuation-target\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"eq:entropy-valuation-target\">[eq:entropy-valuation-target]</a>.*\n\n*The choices of $t_0$ and $\\varepsilon_0(t)$ depend only on $b_0,K_0,B_0,\\zeta$, the finite type and comparison lists, and $(\\omega_s,p_s)_{s\\in\\mathcal T}$. Suppose that $0<v\\le B_0$ and that $W$ is a Boolean function of the actual conjunction predicate satisfying $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\mathbb P_{P_*^t}(W(r)=1)&\\ge b_0vp,\n \\label{eq:entropy-iid-success}\\\\\n \\mathbb P_{\\boldsymbol s}(W(r)=1)\n &\\le v\\bigl(K_0p_{\\boldsymbol s}+\\varepsilon\\bigr)\n \\quad\\text{for every type pattern }\\boldsymbol s,\n \\label{eq:entropy-pattern-upper}\\\\\n \\mathbb P\\bigl(W(r)>W(r')\\bigr)&\\le\\varepsilon v\n \\quad\\text{for every lifted comparison.}\n \\label{eq:entropy-lifted-order}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Then there exists a single Boolean valuation $H$ satisfying Equation <a href=\"#eq:entropy-valuation-target\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"eq:entropy-valuation-target\">[eq:entropy-valuation-target]</a>.*\n\n*The choices of $t_0$ and $\\varepsilon_0(t)$ depend only on $b_0,K_0,B_0,\\zeta$, the finite type and comparison lists, and $(\\omega_s,p_s)_{s\\in\\mathcal T}$. They are uniform in $\\mathcal X$, the primitive supports, their atom probabilities, and the particular comparison couplings with the prescribed marginals. Types with $p_s=0$ are allowed.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Uniform sparsest-cut objective** ([source](#eq:uniform-objective)): For a finite undirected graph $G$ on $[N]$, with nonnegative rational capacities $c_{ij}=c_{ji}$ and $c_{ii}=0$, define $$\label{eq:uniform-objective} \Phi(G)=\min_{\varnothing\ne S\subsetneq[N]} \frac{\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S,\,j\notin S}c_{ij}} {\|S\|(N-\|S\|)}.$$
- **Laurent-series order** ([source](#pm-h-11)): For a formal Laurent series, its order is the least exponent with nonzero coefficient; the order of zero is $+\infty$.
- **Integral Laurent series** ([source](#pm-h-11)): An element of $k((z))$ is called integral if it belongs to $k[[z]]$.
- **Coefficient determinant order** ([source](#pm-h-13)): If $C$ is a basis matrix of this lattice, define its coefficient determinant order by $\delta=\operatorname{ord}_t\det C$.
- **Principal minor** ([source](#pm-h-23)): For $I\subseteq[n]$, define $p_I(T)=\det T_{I,I}$, including $p_\varnothing=1$.
- **Predicate inclusion** ([source](#sec:events)): We write $R\subseteq R'$ when $R(y)\leq R'(y)$ for every $y\in\Omega$.
- **Primitive type** ([source](#sec:events)): A *primitive type* is a probability law $P_s$ on predicates, together with a specified number $p_s\in[0,1]$.
- **Ordered comparison** ([source](#sec:events)): An *ordered comparison* is a coupling of two specified primitive laws supported on pairs $R\subseteq R'$.
- **Calibrated and ordered Boolean valuation** ([source](#stmt-auto-definition-1)): A Boolean valuation $H$ on the actual predicates is *$\zeta$-calibrated and ordered* if, for every primitive type and every specified comparison, $$\label{eq:primitive-calibration-order} \abs{\mathbb E_{R\sim P_s}H(R)-p_s}\leq\zeta, \qquad \mathbb P[H(R)>H(R')]\leq\zeta.$$
- **Score** ([source](#sec:scores)): A *score* is a real function on the cube of assignments to the bit positions of the test system.
- **Measurable lookup** ([source](#sec:scores)): A lookup $h$ is a measurable function from scores to $\{0,1\}$; measurability means Borel measurability on each finite table of score values.
- **Raw comparison form** ([source](#sec:scores)): A raw comparison form is a finite positive measure on pairs of scores, evaluated as $$\mathcal D(h)=\int \|h(B)-h(B')\|\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m(B,B').$$
- **Canonical key** ([source](#pm-h-53)): A *key* is a grid-valued function on the bit cube, specified by its ordered list of essential bit names and its truth table.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-pencil-list-length | Evaluation pencil length | = | $O(d)$ | The implicit constant may depend on the fixed field parameter. |
| q-pencil-construction-time | Deterministic pencil construction time | = | $2^{O(d)}$ | The field parameter is fixed independently of dimension. |
| q-simple-pole-discriminant | Order of the integral-basis discriminant | = | $2(r-1)$ | Order is measured in the base parameter. |
| q-matrix-walk-centered-norm | Matrix-walk norm on centered functions | ≤ | $\lambda_0+q^{-1}$ | The pencil and the field determine the bound. |
| q-masked-pair-centered-norm | Masked pair-operator norm on centered functions | ≤ | $\max\{\lambda_1,\,2/(q-3)\}$ | The bound combines the spatial walk and mask action. |
| q-correction-changed-mass | Fraction of matrix points changed by correction | ≤ | $2\delta$ | The line failure probability and all displayed smallness conditions are required. |
| q-line-support-density | Support density of a nonzero low-line-degree function | ≥ | $1-\rho-\frac{D}{q-1}-\lambda_1$ | The support alternative applies when the function is nonzero. |
| q-analytic-truth-cost | Total raw comparison truth cost | ≤ | $4$ | All construction parameters are fixed independently of formula size. |
| q-perfect-witness-positive-tail | Positive demand-score tail probability | ≥ | $10^{-4}$ | The positive tail starts at the stated positive score threshold. |
| q-amplitude-retained-energy | Squared norm of the initial conditional amplitude signal | ≥ | $c\nu$ | The signal constant depends only on the fixed score-gap parameter. |
| q-capacity-excess-budget | Aggregate rational capacity excess | ≤ | $1$ | The grid and all numerical patterns are fixed before input-dependent enumeration. |
| q-retained-demand-total | Total retained rational demand mass | ≥ | $1-10^{-6}$ | Positive retained masses have an inverse-polynomial lower bound. |
| q-final-output-threshold | Output graph decision threshold | = | $\frac{A_0}{N^2}$ | The fixed hardness factor determines the construction constants; the vertex count determines the threshold. |
| q-variance-transfer | Counting cut variance | ≤ | $36\nu+L_{\rm aux}$ | The auxiliary term measures disagreement charged by tether edges. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-simple-pole-local-model** [claim](#stmt-lem-pencil-simple-pole) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pencil-simple-pole): Formal reparameterization constructs the extension directly; distinct leading orders give its integral basis, and a Vandermonde calculation gives the discriminant. -> An irreducible local extension with an explicit integral basis and discriminant order.
- **step-evaluation-pencil** [claim](#stmt-lem-pencil) / [proof](#pm-h-20): Classify tower branches, bound local lattice determinants, solve finite integrality conditions, and evaluate global functions. A norm-degree estimate bounds quadratic zero evaluations. -> A deterministic evaluation pencil containing the standard basis with the required zero bound and construction time.
- **step-matrix-walk-spectrum** [claim](#stmt-lem-matrix-walk) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-matrix-walk): Diagonalize by additive characters, express each directional character through a matrix of quadratic evaluations, and apply the pencil zero bound. -> A centered operator-norm bound and an internal-edge estimate.
- **step-masked-parity-mixing** [claim](#stmt-lem-masked-pair-mixing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-masked-pair-mixing): Condition on a masked position, separate spatial and multiplicative-mask actions, and bound the omitted mask permutations. -> The pair-operator mixing bound for the exchangeable parity law.
- **step-principal-minor-slack** [claim](#stmt-lem-minor-product-slack) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-minor-product-slack): Use row expansions to move or delete an index in a product of principal minors, then telescope to canonical principal minors and cancel them on Boolean diagonals. -> An explicit decomposition into the permitted slack terms.
- **step-exact-quadratic-correction** [claim](#stmt-lem-quadratic-correction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-quadratic-correction): Enlarge the bad-point set using the internal-edge estimate, establish clean four-position relations, and fill missing points by consistent modal interpolation. -> A nearby function with exact quadratic restrictions at every base along a common high-mass direction set.
- **step-low-degree-support-gap** [claim](#stmt-lem-line-support-gap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-line-support-gap): Start at a support point, use the polynomial root bound on good scalar directions, and average with the internal-edge estimate. -> The support-density lower bound for every nonzero such function.
- **step-bit-test-completeness-soundness** [claim](#stmt-thm-bit-test-system) / [proof](#pm-h-26): Pack assignment, gate, and slack fields with selectors. Correct almost passing lines, force exact bank affinity and invariance using support gaps, and decode a satisfying assignment from Boolean diagonals. -> A polynomially enumerable bounded-arity bit-test system with perfect completeness, constant soundness, and mixing parity queries.
- **step-valuation-obstruction** [claim](#stmt-lem-event-obstruction) / [proof](#pm-h-31): Construct literal and match predicates from the tests. Derive the tag–dictator dichotomy, exclude the tag regime by symmetric sign factorization, and decode ordinary test answers. -> A primitive predicate system whose calibrated ordered valuations are impossible on unsatisfiable instances.
- **step-rare-conjunction-extraction** [claim](#stmt-lem-rare-event) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rare-event): Condition on conjunction success, expose a common prefix density, cancel prefix likelihoods in comparison bounds, control both clipping losses, and threshold to select a common valuation. -> A calibrated ordered valuation with constants independent of supports and atom probabilities.
- **step-amplitude-signal-extraction** [claim](#stmt-lem-amplitude-signal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-amplitude-signal): Apply erasure contraction and orthogonal product projections. Low resampling cost controls high active-set mass, and random half-freezing captures an amplitude component. -> A conditional amplitude signal with bounded total component energy and positive retained energy.
- **step-score-gap** [claim](#stmt-thm-score-gap) / [proof](#pm-h-50): Fund smoothing and replacement comparisons sequentially, retain an amplitude signal at a good stage, represent coarse responses using deleted-slot kernels, and threshold path widths to invoke rare-event extraction. -> The variance-relative analytic score gap against every binary measurable lookup on an unsatisfiable instance.
- **step-finite-numerical-patterns** [claim](#stmt-prop-score-compilation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-score-compilation): Separate discrete seeds, amplitude atoms, and numerical boxes. Enumerate bounded local tables and collision patterns to obtain a fixed finite family of rational polynomial densities and score entries. -> Polynomial seed enumeration, finite numerical polynomial patterns, and lower bounds on positive rounded masses.
- **step-rational-compilation** [claim](#stmt-lem-rational-compilation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rational-compilation): Subdivide rational boxes, resolve rounding with exact polynomial and Lipschitz bounds, integrate resolved boxes exactly, discard unresolved demand, and overcharge unresolved comparison pairs. -> Rational upper capacities and retained lower demand masses with polynomial encodings and controlled aggregate errors.
- **step-unit-demand-hardness** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#pm-h-56): Replicate retained masses into vertex clusters, enforce cluster coherence, and tether remaining keys. Perfect witnesses give cheap threshold cuts; any putative cheap cut transfers counting variance to demand variance and contradicts the score gap. -> A polynomial-time satisfiability reduction to rational-capacity graphs with exactly unit all-pairs demands and the required approximation gap.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **invariant-replication-weights**: Replication converts normalized retained demand into positive integer cluster sizes, with counting weights bounded above and below by the retained weights on their positive support.
- **invariant-auxiliary-mass**: Keys with zero retained demand receive copies, and their total counting mass satisfies the prescribed auxiliary-mass bound.
- **invariant-tether-capacity**: Each auxiliary key is joined to each positive retained-demand key with the prescribed nonnegative rational tether capacity.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-use-finite-check-as-hardness-certificate**: Treat successful replication checks as certification of the complete approximation-hardness reduction. -> The executable construction checks cluster sizes and tether capacities; it does not establish the algebraic tests, analytic score gap, or satisfiability reduction.
- **anti-use-omit-zero-mass-keys**: Remove keys whose retained demand is zero or omit their tethers. -> Their possible contribution to counting demand would lose the coupling penalty used in the soundness argument.
- **anti-use-arbitrary-replication-scale**: Choose a convenient replication scale without checking the support and size lower bounds. -> Rounding can then violate the counting-weight or auxiliary-mass bounds needed to transfer demand.
- **anti-use-nonuniform-demand-substitution**: Interpret the tether edges as the demand pairs of the output graph. -> Tethers are capacities; restricting demand to those edges changes the objective.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The finite replication and tether construction is explicit: cluster sizes are prescribed by rounding normalized retained demand, and tether capacities are rational products. This executable fragment checks that interface only. Source: “Every key has a positive number of copies.”

```python
# INPUTS: JSON containing masses as rational strings, integer K, and rational-string kappa.
# PRECONDITIONS: Nonnegative masses sum to one; kappa is positive; K is at least four times kappa times the number of keys and at least every reciprocal positive mass.
# OUTPUTS: JSON cluster sizes n and tether triples [auxiliary index, positive index, rational capacity].
# TIME: Quadratic in the number of keys, apart from exact rational arithmetic.
# SPACE: Quadratic in the number of keys for the emitted tether list.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact integers and fractions.
# FAILURE: Invalid contracts raise an exception and produce no accepted result.
import json
import sys
from fractions import Fraction

def construct(data):
    masses = [Fraction(x) for x in data['masses']]
    kappa = Fraction(data['kappa'])
    scale = data['K']
    if type(scale) is not int or scale <= 0:
        raise ValueError('invalid replication scale')
    if not masses or any(x < 0 for x in masses) or sum(masses) != 1 or kappa <= 0:
        raise ValueError('invalid retained demand')
    if scale < 4 * kappa * len(masses):
        raise ValueError('insufficient size bound')
    if any(scale * x < 1 for x in masses if x > 0):
        raise ValueError('insufficient support bound')
    n = []
    for mass in masses:
        if mass == 0:
            n.append(1)
        else:
            scaled = scale * mass
            n.append((scaled.numerator + scaled.denominator - 1) // scaled.denominator)
    total = sum(n)
    tethers = []
    for i, mass in enumerate(masses):
        if mass == 0:
            for j, target in enumerate(masses):
                if target > 0:
                    capacity = 2 * kappa * Fraction(n[i], total) * masses[j]
                    tethers.append([i, j, str(capacity)])
    return {'n': n, 'tethers': tethers}

if __name__ == '__main__':
    print(json.dumps(construct(json.load(sys.stdin)), sort_keys=True))
```

1 < K * mass <= count\n        else:\n            assert count == 1\n    N = sum(n)\n    assert K <= N <= K + len(p)\n    w = [Fraction(count, N) for count in n]\n    for mass, weight in zip(p, w):\n        if mass:\n            assert mass / 2 <= weight <= 2 * mass\n    auxiliary = sum(weight for mass, weight in zip(p, w) if not mass)\n    assert auxiliary <= 1 / (4 * kappa)\n    edges = {}\n    assert type(out['tethers']) is list\n    for triple in out['tethers']:\n        assert type(triple) is list and len(triple) == 3\n        i, j, text = triple\n        assert type(i) is int and type(j) is int\n        assert 0 <= i < len(p) and 0 <= j < len(p)\n        assert not p[i] and p[j] > 0\n        assert (i, j) not in edges\n        capacity = Fraction(text)\n        assert capacity >= 0\n        # Verify capacity through a cross-multiplied conservation equation.\n        assert capacity * N == (kappa + kappa) * n[i] * p[j]\n        edges[i, j] = capacity\n    required = {(i, j) for i in range(len(p)) for j in range(len(p)) if not p[i] and p[j] > 0}\n    assert set(edges) == required\n    # Exhaustively check the tether disagreement identity on every key coloring.\n    for colors in itertools.product((False, True), repeat=len(p)):\n        actual = sum(cap for (i, j), cap in edges.items() if colors[i] != colors[j])\n        disagreement = sum(w[i] * sum(p[j] for j in range(len(p)) if colors[i] != colors[j]) for i in range(len(p)) if not p[i])\n        assert actual == (kappa + kappa) * disagreement\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    try:\n        check()\n    except Exception:\n        sys.exit(1)\n",

    "reason": "The finite replication and tether construction is explicit: cluster sizes are prescribed by rounding normalized retained demand, and tether capacities are rational products. This executable fragment checks that interface only. Source: “Every key has a positive number of copies.”",
    "reference_code": "# INPUTS: JSON containing masses as rational strings, integer K, and rational-string kappa.\n# PRECONDITIONS: Nonnegative masses sum to one; kappa is positive; K is at least four times kappa times the number of keys and at least every reciprocal positive mass.\n# OUTPUTS: JSON cluster sizes n and tether triples [auxiliary index, positive index, rational capacity].\n# TIME: Quadratic in the number of keys, apart from exact rational arithmetic.\n# SPACE: Quadratic in the number of keys for the emitted tether list.\n# ARITHMETIC: Exact integers and fractions.\n# FAILURE: Invalid contracts raise an exception and produce no accepted result.\nimport json\nimport sys\nfrom fractions import Fraction\n\ndef construct(data):\n    masses = [Fraction(x) for x in data['masses']]\n    kappa = Fraction(data['kappa'])\n    scale = data['K']\n    if type(scale) is not int or scale <= 0:\n        raise ValueError('invalid replication scale')\n    if not masses or any(x < 0 for x in masses) or sum(masses) != 1 or kappa <= 0:\n        raise ValueError('invalid retained demand')\n    if scale < 4 * kappa * len(masses):\n        raise ValueError('insufficient size bound')\n    if any(scale * x < 1 for x in masses if x > 0):\n        raise ValueError('insufficient support bound')\n    n = []\n    for mass in masses:\n        if mass == 0:\n            n.append(1)\n        else:\n            scaled = scale * mass\n            n.append((scaled.numerator + scaled.denominator - 1) // scaled.denominator)\n    total = sum(n)\n    tethers = []\n    for i, mass in enumerate(masses):\n        if mass == 0:\n            for j, target in enumerate(masses):\n                if target > 0:\n                    capacity = 2 * kappa * Fraction(n[i], total) * masses[j]\n                    tethers.append([i, j, str(capacity)])\n    return {'n': n, 'tethers': tethers}\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    print(json.dumps(construct(json.load(sys.stdin)), sort_keys=True))\n",

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The finite compilation supplies a concrete representation pipeline for bounded-arity score functions: round their tables, remove inessential bit names, and identify equal functions by canonical keys. This can support software that represents the reduction, although the fixed numerical parameters may be extremely large. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The constructed scores are functions of proof assignments, and their comparison measures serve a satisfiability reduction. The paper supplies no game dynamics, physical state evolution, or simulation accuracy contract. |
| tools | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Rational compilation gives a concrete method for producing conservative capacity and demand bounds from the prescribed finite families of polynomial score experiments. Its useful contract is directional: capacities are upper bounds and retained demands are lower bounds. |
| algorithms | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The quadratic evaluation pencil is an explicit deterministic construction. It produces a short evaluation list containing the standard basis and bounds zeros only for homogeneous quadratic polynomials that are nonzero somewhere on that list. |
| data_structures | True | strong | implementable | inferred | Canonical keys provide an explicit finite data representation that identifies score functions by their essential arguments and values. Replication then associates each key with a cluster and a designated representative. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The construction specifies finite-field arithmetic and bit-operation bounds, but no accelerator architecture, parallel memory layout, or hardware throughput guarantee. A sequential complexity bound does not supply a hardware implementation contract. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The bounded-query laws test proof assignments with completeness, soundness, and mixing properties. The paper does not turn these laws into a cryptographic protocol or establish adversarial security, secrecy, or cryptographic parameter selection. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The analytic lookup is an arbitrary measurable Boolean function on scores. The argument establishes a comparison-cost obstruction for such functions, without a training procedure, statistical learning model, or prediction guarantee. |
| optimisation_operations_research | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | The paper constructively generates finite rational graph instances with exactly uniform demands and a satisfiability-dependent approximation gap. This supports theoretical evaluation of approximation guarantees for the stated objective; it does not provide a sparsest-cut optimiser. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The graph objective can resemble a communication partitioning objective, but the paper gives no distributed protocol, communication-round analysis, failure model, or consistency mechanism. Its concrete output is a centrally constructed capacity graph. |
| other (computational_complexity) | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The main construction is an unconditional polynomial-time reduction from satisfiability to an approximation gap for Uniform Sparsest Cut. Its contract includes rational encodings and all nonempty proper cuts, while permitting dependence on the fixed approximation factor. |

          "exact_quote": "For every fixed $\\lambda>0$ there are constants $\\eta>0$, $B<\\infty$, and a fixed finite list of bounded-arity test laws with the following properties. All samplers are polynomially enumerable finite weighted lists with rational probabilities; every positive seed probability is at least inverse-polynomial in the input size.*\n\n2.  *If the formula is satisfiable, there is an assignment $y:\\mathcal S\\to\\mathbb F_2$ accepted by every test in every support.*\n\n3.  *If the formula is unsatisfiable, every assignment fails one of the test laws with probability greater than $\\eta$.*\n\n4.  *There is also an exchangeable law of $(s_1,s_2,s_3,s_4)\\in\\mathcal S^4$, with uniform marginals, such that the conditional expectation operator of any coordinate given any other has norm at most $\\lambda$ on mean-zero $L^2(\\mathcal S)$. <a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-fixed-approximation-factor** (formal_scope): The approximation factor is fixed before the input varies. Polynomial size and running time do not imply a polynomial exponent uniform in the approximation factor. Blocked use: ['Treating the reduction as a uniform efficient construction when the approximation factor grows with input size.', 'Inferring practical construction sizes from asymptotic polynomial bounds.']
- **limit-uniform-undirected-objective** (formal_scope): The hardness statement concerns finite undirected graphs with nonnegative rational capacities, binary encodings, and unit demand between all distinct vertex pairs. Blocked use: ['Transferring the theorem directly to directed cuts, selected-pair demand objectives, or a different normalization.', 'Claiming that evaluating an already supplied cut is the approximation search problem.']
- **limit-pencil-evaluation-nonvanishing** (hypothesis): The quadratic zero bound requires nonvanishing somewhere on the constructed evaluation list. Formal nonzeroness of a polynomial alone is insufficient. Blocked use: ['Applying the zero bound to a homogeneous quadratic that vanishes at every listed vector.']
- **limit-pencil-dimension-cost** (formal_scope): The pencil construction has exponential bit complexity in its ambient dimension. Polynomial dependence on formula size uses the logarithmic address dimension chosen in the reduction. Blocked use: ['Claiming polynomial construction time for arbitrary ambient dimensions.', 'Allowing the field parameter to vary while retaining the fixed-parameter bounds.']
- **limit-local-simple-pole** (hypothesis): The local irreducibility, integral basis, and discriminant assertions assume a simple pole in the specified Laurent series field. Blocked use: ['Applying the same local extension description to integral right sides or arbitrary higher-order poles.']
- **limit-test-system-sampling-contract** (hypothesis): The bit-test construction uses its prescribed finite rational sampling laws, controlled question marginals, and auxiliary mixing parity law. Soundness is not a property of arbitrary bounded-query tests. Blocked use: ['Replacing the correlated parity sampler by an arbitrary exchangeable sampler.', 'Discarding the query marginal bound when transferring errors to decoded assignments.']
- **limit-slack-boolean-diagonals** (hypothesis): The slack decomposition applies to linear combinations of products of principal minors that vanish on every Boolean diagonal matrix. Blocked use: ['Applying the decomposition to unrestricted polynomials without establishing the specified product representation and diagonal vanishing.']
- **limit-correction-smallness** (hypothesis): Exact quadratic correction requires both the line-failure bound and all displayed smallness inequalities. Its conclusion concerns a common high-mass direction set and allows changes to the original function. Blocked use: ['Claiming exact quadratic restrictions in every direction.', 'Claiming that the unmodified function already satisfies every corrected line identity.', 'Applying correction without checking all smallness conditions.']
- **limit-support-gap-alternative** (edge_case): The support lower bound has an identically zero alternative and assumes scalar invariance, sufficient direction mass, and the stated strict line-degree bound. Blocked use: ['Giving the zero function a positive support lower bound.', 'Using the root-count argument when the degree restriction or scalar invariance is missing.']
- **limit-actual-function-identity** (hypothesis): Responses must be functions of actual predicates or scores. Different sampling presentations of the same function cannot receive different answers. Blocked use: ['Using presentation labels to distinguish equal predicates.', 'Letting a score coloring depend on sampling metadata omitted from the score function.']
- **limit-rare-event-joint-requirements** (hypothesis): Rare-event extraction requires the mixture success lower bound, every type-pattern upper bound, and every lifted comparison bound for a common response. The repetition length and tolerance must be chosen after the fixed numerical data. Blocked use: ['Extracting a valuation from success probability alone.', 'Replacing patternwise bounds by only an averaged bound.', 'Using different responses for different types or comparisons.']
- **limit-rare-event-existential-selection** (nonconstructive): The rare-event proof selects a prefix by an expected total-error bound. This existence argument does not itself supply an efficient decoder for an arbitrary conjunction response. Blocked use: ['Publishing the prefix-selection argument as a polynomial-time decoding algorithm without an additional effective representation and search procedure.']
- **limit-score-positive-variance** (edge_case): The analytic soundness inequality is stated for binary measurable lookups of positive demand variance on NO instances. A constant coloring does not meet that premise. Blocked use: ['Dividing comparison cost by zero variance.', 'Applying the NO-instance inequality to arbitrary real-valued or nonmeasurable lookups.']
- **limit-amplitude-resampling-stage-size** (hypothesis): Amplitude signal extraction depends on the specified slot-resampling disagreement bound, random half-freezing experiment, and stage-size lower bounds. Blocked use: ['Claiming a retained amplitude signal for an arbitrary lookup without the resampling bound.', 'Changing the freeze experiment or undersizing stages while retaining the same capture estimate.']
- **limit-sequential-parameter-funding** (hypothesis): The parameter schedule is sequential. Later stage sizes and smoothing scales are chosen only after the earlier partitions and funding quantities are fixed. Blocked use: ['Choosing all stage parameters independently and assuming the comparison truth-cost budgets remain valid.', 'Treating lookup-dependent reference signs as inputs to the comparison samplers.']
- **limit-deleted-background-and-rebuilding** (hypothesis): The common amplitude response uses preceding partitions and a deleted-slot background. The extended reference-sign independence applies only to the tested subbatch, and forcing a trial rebuilds its fine state. Blocked use: ['Retaining an old fine partition after replacing the forced path data.', 'Asserting unconditional mutual independence after mixing over the exterior background.', 'Extending reference-sign independence to every unfrozen slot.']
- **limit-fixed-polynomial-patterns** (formal_scope): Finite compilation relies on a fixed finite family of numerical polynomial patterns with bounded degree and fixed numerical dimension, after the approximation factor and construction parameters are fixed. Blocked use: ['Claiming the same polynomial complexity for numerical families whose dimensions, degrees, or pattern counts grow with the input.', 'Replacing the prescribed conditional numerical laws by arbitrary real integrals.']
- **limit-directional-rational-approximation** (approximation): Rational compilation rounds score tables deterministically, integrates resolved polynomial boxes exactly, discards unresolved demand, and overcharges unresolved comparison pairs. Its guarantee is directional rather than exact integration. Blocked use: ['Using symmetric numerical error estimates as substitutes for upper capacities and lower demand masses.', 'Adding conditional error bounds without their seed probabilities.', 'Ignoring polynomial entries identically equal to a rounding boundary.']
- **limit-small-demand-and-auxiliary-keys** (hypothesis): Retaining small cuts requires multiplicative demand domination and auxiliary tethers. An additive demand approximation alone does not establish final soundness. Blocked use: ['Dropping zero retained-demand keys or omitting their tethers.', 'Replacing the final demand transfer by an additive approximation that can overwhelm a small cut.']
- **limit-replication-and-coherence** (hypothesis): Replication requires both lower bounds on the replication scale. Coherence edges are essential to force low-ratio cuts to be monochromatic on every cluster. Blocked use: ['Choosing an arbitrary replication scale.', 'Applying key-coloring soundness to a split cluster without accounting for its coherence-edge capacity.']
- **limit-finite-replication-certificate-scope** (formal_scope): Checking replication arithmetic and tether identities establishes only that finite interface. The full hardness theorem also requires the algebraic test system, valuation obstruction, analytic score gap, and rational compilation. Blocked use: ['Treating agreement on finite replication examples as verification of the complete satisfiability reduction.', 'Treating a local arithmetic certificate as a proof of approximation hardness.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that, for every fixed <i>C</i> &gt; 1, approximating Uniform Sparsest Cut within factor <i>C</i> is NP-hard. The output graphs have nonnegative rational capacities and unit demand between every pair of distinct vertices.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:uniform-objective"></a>
For a finite undirected graph $G$ on $[N]$, with nonnegative rational capacities $c_{ij}=c_{ji}$ and $c_{ii}=0$, define $$\label{eq:uniform-objective}
 \Phi(G)=\min_{\varnothing\ne S\subsetneq[N]}
 \frac{\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S,\,j\notin S}c_{ij}}
 {|S|(N-|S|)}.$$ The denominator is the number of unordered pairs separated by the cut. Thus Equation <a href="#eq:uniform-objective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-objective">[eq:uniform-objective]</a> is the sparsest-cut objective with demand exactly one on every pair of distinct vertices. Capacities and thresholds are encoded in binary. A factor-$C$ approximation must return a nonempty proper cut whose ratio is at most $C\Phi(G)$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every fixed real constant $C>1$, there is a polynomial-time reduction from $3$CNF satisfiability to finite undirected graphs with nonnegative rational capacities and exactly unit demands, together with a positive rational threshold $a$, such that $$\begin{array}{ll}
 \text{the formula is satisfiable}&\Longrightarrow\ \Phi(G)\le a,\\[2pt]
 \text{the formula is unsatisfiable}&\Longrightarrow\ \Phi(G)>Ca.
 \end{array}$$ The graph size and the bit lengths of all output numbers are polynomial in the formula size, with constants and exponents allowed to depend on $C$. In particular, approximating Uniform Sparsest Cut within any fixed factor $C>1$ is NP-hard.*

</div>

The source decision problem is NP-complete (Karp 1972, Main Theorem, Problem 11) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Karp1972](#ref-Karp1972)]. The reduction uses no unproved complexity hypothesis. It gives an explicit gap for the objective in Equation <a href="#eq:uniform-objective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-objective">[eq:uniform-objective]</a>, including every nonempty proper cut, however small either side may be.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Historical context and significance

Uniform Sparsest Cut asks for a partition that separates little capacity relative to the number of vertex pairs it separates. This normalization makes the problem sensitive to bottlenecks at every scale, including cuts with a small side. Its connection with multicommodity flow gives both a way to certify that a graph has no sparse cut and a route to approximation algorithms. Leighton and Rao developed this flow–cut approach and its logarithmic approximation guarantees (Leighton and Rao 1999) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[LeightonRao1999](#ref-LeightonRao1999)]. Arora, Rao, and Vazirani improved the approximation factor to $O(\sqrt{\log N})$ using semidefinite programming; their treatment of the unweighted uniform objective appears in (Arora, Rao, and Vazirani 2009, sec. 6, Theorem 16) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[ARV2009](#ref-ARV2009)].

Exact hardness and approximation hardness address different questions. Matula and Shahrokhi established NP-hardness of finding sparsest cuts (Matula and Shahrokhi 1990) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[MatulaShahrokhi1990](#ref-MatulaShahrokhi1990)]; Bonsma, Broersma, Patel, and Pyatkin proved NP-completeness for unweighted graphs with the cardinality-product denominator used here (Bonsma et al. 2012, Theorem 15) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[BBPP2012](#ref-BBPP2012)]. To rule out a constant-factor approximation, one must create a multiplicative separation between satisfiable and unsatisfiable instances, while preserving the uniform denominator.

Previous approximation lower bounds depend on the complexity assumption and on which pairs carry demand. Ambühl, Mastrolilli, and Svensson proved that a polynomial-time approximation scheme for Uniform Sparsest Cut would yield probabilistic SAT algorithms of time $2^{n^\varepsilon}$ for every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, where $n$ is the SAT input length (Ambühl, Mastrolilli, and Svensson 2011, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[AMS2011](#ref-AMS2011)]. Raghavendra and Steurer introduced the Small-Set Expansion hypothesis and its connection with Unique Games (Raghavendra and Steurer 2010) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[RaghavendraSteurer2010](#ref-RaghavendraSteurer2010)]. Raghavendra, Steurer, and Tulsiani established arbitrarily large constant-factor gaps for Balanced Separator from that hypothesis (Raghavendra, Steurer, and Tulsiani 2012, full version, Theorem 3.5 and Corollary 3.6) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[RST2012](#ref-RST2012)]. Their balanced-cut statement measures set size by stationary volume. The standard recursive reduction then yields hardness of every constant factor for Uniform Sparsest Cut under polynomial-time oracle reductions, assuming the same hypothesis.[^1]

The nonuniform problem permits a selected set $\mathcal D$ of distinct demand pairs. In the formulation of Chawla, Krauthgamer, Kumar, Rabani, and Sivakumar, a solution is an edge set $M$ with total capacity $c(M)$, and its ratio is $c(M)/q_\mathcal D(M)$, where $q_\mathcal D(M)>0$ counts the demand pairs disconnected after deleting $M$. They proved every-constant-factor hardness under the Unique Games Conjecture (Chawla et al. 2006, Corollary 1.3 and p. 99) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[CKKRS2006](#ref-CKKRS2006)]. The independent Unique Games Theorem supplies the required game premise: its unweighted bipartite constraints can be given equal weights summing to one (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 8.3) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[OpenAIUniqueGames2026](#ref-OpenAIUniqueGames2026)]. The resulting nonuniform hardness concerns cutset-output search under polynomial-time Cook reductions. Only the selected pairs carry unit demand; they need not include all distinct vertex pairs. The direct reduction proving Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is independent of this nonuniform consequence.

Khot and Vishnoi also developed the connection between Unique Games, cut hardness, and negative-type metrics (Khot and Vishnoi 2015) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[KhotVishnoi2015](#ref-KhotVishnoi2015)]. Ordinary NP-hardness for nonuniform sparsest cut is known even with treewidth-two supply graphs, with a $17/16-\varepsilon$ approximation threshold (Gupta, Talwar, and Witmer 2013, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[GuptaTalwarWitmer2013](#ref-GuptaTalwarWitmer2013)]. The separation between the uniform and nonuniform hardness questions is discussed in (Manurangsi and Trevisan 2018, sec. 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[ManurangsiTrevisan2018](#ref-ManurangsiTrevisan2018)] and in the recent work of d’Orsi, Jones, Ruotolo, Vadhan, and Zhang on low-degree Abelian Cayley graphs (d’Orsi et al. 2025) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[DOrsiEtAl2025](#ref-DOrsiEtAl2025)].

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> establishes an unconditional approximation gap with unit demands. Its force comes from controlling all cut masses in a polynomial-size reduction. The proof starts with an explicit algebraic test system for $3$CNF, extracts a Boolean valuation from a rare successful event, and forces that event from any cut whose comparison cost is small relative to its demand variance.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof structure and main constructions

The reduction has four interfaces. A satisfying assignment supplies a low-cost cut; conversely, a cut with small cost relative to its separated demand will determine a satisfying assignment. The main difficulty in this converse is that the separated demand can be arbitrarily small. Errors must therefore be controlled relative to that demand, rather than by one fixed additive tolerance.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Algebraic testing.

A proof is a bit assignment on an explicitly constructed finite set of positions. A test samples a bounded list of positions and checks their answers. Section <a href="#sec:pencil" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pencil">2</a> constructs a linear-size list of evaluation vectors on which a quadratic form that is nonzero somewhere on the list vanishes only rarely. It uses the Garcia–Stichtenoth tower (Garcia and Stichtenoth 1996; Stichtenoth 2001) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[GS1996](#ref-GS1996), [Stichtenoth2001](#ref-Stichtenoth2001)]; the local algebra, dimension estimates, and finite computation needed here are supplied in that section. Section <a href="#sec:bit-tests" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:bit-tests">3</a> uses this list to construct tests whose individual query probabilities are bounded by a fixed multiple of uniform measure. Its auxiliary parity-query law has exactly uniform marginals and strongly mixing pairs. It encodes Boolean tables by matrix minors and corrects a nearly passing oracle so that quadratic line identities hold at every base on a high-mass set of directions. Local proof testing belongs to the PCP framework developed by Arora and Safra and by Arora, Lund, Motwani, Sudan, and Szegedy, and subsequently recast through gap amplification by Dinur (Arora and Safra 1998; Arora et al. 1998; Dinur 2007) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[AroraSafra1998](#ref-AroraSafra1998), [ALMSS1998](#ref-ALMSS1998), [Dinur2007](#ref-Dinur2007)]. The correlated test laws and their soundness are proved here.

A *predicate* is a Boolean function of the entire proof assignment. A *valuation* assigns a bit to each actual predicate, so two sampled descriptions of the same function must receive the same answer. Calibration prescribes its mean under each specified predicate-sampling law, and order preservation asks that it usually respect pointwise inclusions of predicates. Section <a href="#sec:events" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:events">4</a> proves that an approximately calibrated, approximately order-preserving valuation would decode a satisfying assignment.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Rare-event extraction.

Section <a href="#sec:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:entropy">5</a> studies a Boolean response to a conjunction of many independently sampled predicates. Its success probability may be very small. Conditioning on success and exposing a common prefix produces a density with nearly extremal entropy. Comparisons with the constant-one predicate bound the density from above in mean. An explicit clipping argument then forces it near two values, zero and a fixed positive constant. One threshold gives a single valuation satisfying all required calibrations and comparisons. The bounds depend on the fixed list of predicate types, not on their support sizes or on a smallest atom.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### From cut variance to a rare successful event.

A *score* is a bounded real function of boundedly many proof bits. A candidate cut is a measurable $\{0,1\}$-valued function of scores. A demand law measures the variance of this color, and comparison measures charge for disagreement between pairs of scores. Sections <a href="#sec:scores" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:scores">6</a> and <a href="#sec:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:paths">7</a> construct these measures. The demand score is a sum of rare predicates with independent real amplitudes at several scales. Small resampling cost forces the cut color to retain a signal from at least one amplitude. Smoothed comparison paths preserve enough of this signal to produce the successful conjunction response required above. The conditional experiments ensure that this response depends on the predicate itself. Every error is proportional to the cut variance, so the argument applies to every positive demand mass.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### Exact uniform demands.

Section <a href="#sec:finite-graph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite-graph">8</a> rounds each score to a grid-valued truth table. A *key* consists of that table together with its ordered essential bit names. Equal functions have the same key. Box subdivision gives rational upper capacities and rational lower demand masses. Replicating keys converts these retained masses into vertex counts; heavy edges prevent a cut of small ratio from separating copies. Additional edges from the remaining keys charge for their possible contribution to separated demand. This produces exactly the denominator $|S|(N-|S|)$ with polynomially many vertices and polynomial binary encodings.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Conventions and parameter dependence

All probabilities refer to the explicitly described experiments. A predicate is a Boolean function of the entire proof-bit assignment; its sampling data may be retained when conditioning, but a response to the predicate depends only on that function. The same convention applies to scores. Thus two presentations of the same function cannot receive different cut colors.

For a probability law $\mu$ and a Boolean function $h$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\mu(h)=\mu(h=1)\mu(h=0)
 =\tfrac12\mathbb E_{B,B'\sim\mu}\abs{h(B)-h(B')},$$ where the last two draws are independent. This identity explains the variance normalization used before the final graph is formed.

The approximation factor is fixed first. Thereafter every numerical constant, field size, arity, and number of repetitions is fixed independently of the input formula. Parameters chosen later may depend on earlier parameters only; the necessary order is specified in the proofs. Bounds written $O(\cdot)$ have constants depending only on parameters already fixed at that point. The construction is polynomial in the formula size; no claim is made that its exponent is uniform in $C$.

<a id="sec:pencil"></a>
### An explicit pencil of directions

The first ingredient is a short list of vectors on which every quadratic evaluation that is nonzero somewhere on the list has large support. Its length must be linear in the ambient dimension. We construct the list by evaluating functions in an additive tower, and give the local calculations and finite algorithm needed for its uniformity.

<a id="stmt-lem-pencil"></a>
<div id="lem:pencil" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Quadratic evaluation pencil). *For every $0<\lambda_0<1$ and every sufficiently large power of two $r$, put $q=r^2$. There is a deterministic algorithm which, given an integer $d\geq 1$, constructs a list $$u_1,\ldots,u_m\in\mathbb F_q^d$$ with the following properties:*

1.  *$m=O(d)$, and the list contains every standard basis vector of $\mathbb F_q^d$;*

2.  *if a homogeneous quadratic polynomial $Q$ in $d$ variables does not vanish at every point of the list, then $$\#\{j:Q(u_j)=0\}\leq\lambda_0m.$$*

*The running time is $2^{O(d)}$ bit operations. The constants in both bounds may depend on $r$, which is fixed independently of $d$.*

</div>

In Section <a href="#sec:bit-tests" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:bit-tests">3</a>, these vectors define rank-one directions for a random walk on a space of matrices. Its spectral estimate needs only quadratic evaluations that are nonzero somewhere on the list, exactly the hypothesis in <span class="upright">(ii)</span>. The application takes $d=O(\log(\text{input size}+1))$, so the construction time is polynomial in the formula size.

The proof first describes the local branches of the tower and their integral lattices. Their determinant orders bound the conditions imposed on global functions with controlled poles. We then evaluate a sufficiently large space of these functions to obtain the quadratic zero bound, and finally give a finite algorithm with explicit precision and running-time bounds.

<a id="eq:pencil-parameters"></a>
<a id="eq:pencil-tower"></a>
<a id="eq:pencil-power-basis"></a>
Fix a power of two $r$ sufficiently large that, with $D=r+1$, $$\label{eq:pencil-parameters}
 \frac{2D}{r^2-r}\leq\lambda_0.$$ All finite fields used below have characteristic two. Write $F=\mathbb F_q$ and let $k$ be an algebraic closure of $F$. Define $$\label{eq:pencil-tower}
 S(Y)=Y^r+Y,\qquad
 f(Y)=\frac{Y^{r+1}}{Y^r+Y}
     =\frac{Y^r}{Y^{r-1}+1}
     =\frac{1}{S(1/Y)},\qquad
 S(x_{i+1})=f(x_i),$$ where $x_1$ is transcendental. This is the Garcia–Stichtenoth tower (Garcia and Stichtenoth 1996) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[GS1996](#ref-GS1996)]; the recurrence also appears in (Stichtenoth 2001, Example 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Stichtenoth2001](#ref-Stichtenoth2001)]. The identities involving inverses are rational-function identities. In particular, the middle expression defines $f$ at zero. We will prove that the tower through $x_l$ is a field of degree $N=r^{l-1}$ over $k(x_1)$, and that the monomials $$\label{eq:pencil-power-basis}
 e_{\boldsymbol a}=x_2^{a_2}\cdots x_l^{a_l},
 \qquad 0\leq a_i<r,$$ form a basis. For $l=1$ this is the one-element basis $1$. The same equations and basis will then define the tower over $F(x_1)$.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The two local extensions

For a formal Laurent series, its order is the least exponent with nonzero coefficient; the order of zero is $+\infty$. An element of $k((z))$ is called integral if it belongs to $k[[z]]$.

<a id="eq:pencil-additive-recursion"></a>
Suppose first that the right side of an equation $S(X)=b(z)$ becomes integral after subtracting $S(g)$, for a specified $g\in k((z))$. Put $b_0=b-S(g)\in k[[z]]$. There are exactly $r$ choices of a constant $\beta$ satisfying $\beta^r+\beta=b_0(0)$: the derivative of this polynomial is one, and its roots differ by the elements of $\mathbb F_r$. For each choice, there is a unique positive-order series $u$ with $S(u)=b_0-S(\beta)$. Indeed, if $b_0-S(\beta)=\sum_{n>0}b_nz^n$, then $$\label{eq:pencil-additive-recursion}
 u_n=b_n+\mathbf 1_{\{r\mid n\}}u_{n/r}^{r}\qquad(n>0)$$ determines its coefficients successively. Thus the equation splits into $r$ distinct roots $g+\beta+u$ in $k((z))$. Its local algebra is a product of $r$ copies of $k((z))$, with integral lattice $k[[z]]^r$.

The second case is a simple pole. It is useful to establish the parameter and degree assertions without first assuming irreducibility.

<a id="stmt-lem-pencil-simple-pole"></a>
<div id="lem:pencil-simple-pole" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (A simple-pole extension). *Let $b\in k((z))$ have order $-1$. The polynomial $X^r+X-b$ is irreducible over $k((z))$. Its extension is a Laurent series field $k((w))$, where $w=1/X$ and $z$ has order $r$ in $w$. Its integral lattice has the basis $$1,w,\ldots,w^{r-1}\quad\text{over }k[[z]],$$ and the discriminant of this basis has $z$-order $2(r-1)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pencil-simple-pole"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pencil-simple-pole</summary>

<a id="eq:pencil-reparameterization"></a>
*Proof.* The series $q_0(z)=1/b(z)$ has order one. Its compositional inverse $\psi$ exists: the coefficient of each new term is determined by division by the nonzero linear coefficient of $q_0$. In a new series field $k((w))$, set $$\label{eq:pencil-reparameterization}
 z=\psi\left(\frac{w^r}{1+w^{r-1}}\right).$$ Substitution gives an injective homomorphism from $k((z))$ to $k((w))$, and $z=cw^r+\cdots$ for some $c\ne0$. Moreover $X=1/w$ satisfies $X^r+X=b(z)$.

The monomials $w^jz^n$, with $0\leq j<r$ and $n\geq0$, have distinct leading orders $j+rn$, which exhaust the nonnegative integers. Given a power series in $w$, subtract its lowest remaining coefficient using the unique such monomial of that order, and repeat. The subtractions converge formally because their orders tend to infinity. Collecting terms with the same $j$ gives $$k[[w]]=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{r-1}w^j k[[z]].$$ The sum is direct: the leading orders of nonzero summands belong to distinct residue classes modulo $r$. After multiplying a Laurent series by a sufficiently large power of $z$, the same argument applies. Hence $[k((w)):k((z))]=r$ and $k((w))=k((z))(w)=k((z))(X)$. This proves irreducibility, and separability follows from the derivative $S'(X)=1$.

The displayed free module is closed under multiplication, so each of its elements is integral over $k[[z]]$ by the characteristic polynomial of its multiplication map. Conversely, an element of negative $w$-order cannot satisfy a monic polynomial over $k[[z]]$: its highest power would be the unique term of smallest order. Thus $k[[w]]$ is precisely the integral closure.

The $r$ embeddings send $X$ to $X+c$, $c\in\mathbb F_r$, and therefore send $w$ to $w_c=1/(X+c)$. For distinct $c,c'$, the difference $$w_c-w_{c'}=\frac{c'-c}{(X+c)(X+c')}$$ has $w$-order two, or extended $z$-order $2/r$. The evaluation matrix of $1,w,\ldots,w^{r-1}$ is Vandermonde. Its determinant squared is the trace-pairing determinant, so the latter has order $2\binom r2(2/r)=2(r-1)$. ◻

</details>

Here and below the trace of an element of a finite algebra is the trace of its multiplication map, and its norm is the determinant of that map. For a separable field or product of separable fields, passing to a field containing all embeddings diagonalizes multiplication. Thus trace is the sum of the embedded values, and the trace matrix of a basis is the transpose of its evaluation matrix times that matrix. This also proves the discriminant identity used in Lemma <a href="#lem:pencil-simple-pole" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pencil-simple-pole">3</a>.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### All branches of the tower

At a pole of $Y$, the difference $f(Y)-Y$ is integral: from $f(Y)=Y/(1+Y^{1-r})$, its order is at least zero. Start at infinity with the parameter $t=1/x_1$. The first right side has a simple pole, and Lemma <a href="#lem:pencil-simple-pole" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pencil-simple-pole">3</a> produces a single branch in which $x_2$ has a simple pole with parameter $1/x_2$. Repeating the lemma gives a single branch at every level. Inductively, the polynomial defining $x_{i+1}$ is irreducible over this completion of the preceding field, and hence over that field itself. This proves the degree and basis assertions following Equation <a href="#eq:pencil-power-basis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:pencil-power-basis">[eq:pencil-power-basis]</a>. It also proves them over $F(x_1)$, since an equation irreducible after extending constants to $k$ was already irreducible over $F$.

At a finite point $x_1=a$, put $t=x_1-a$. If $a\in\mathbb F_r\setminus\{0\}$, then $S(x_1)$ has a simple zero and $x_1^{r+1}$ is a unit. Thus the first right side has a simple pole, and all subsequent steps are again simple-pole extensions. If $a\notin\mathbb F_r$, the first right side is integral and the extension splits. An integral residue $\beta\notin\mathbb F_r$ has $f(\beta)\ne0$, so no root of $S(Y)=f(\beta)$ belongs to $\mathbb F_r$. Induction therefore gives only unshifted integral splittings at such a point.

<a id="eq:pencil-first-shift"></a>
It remains to describe every branch above zero. As long as the residues of $x_1,x_2,\ldots$ are zero, all extensions split without a shift. Since $f(Y)$ has order $r$ at zero and $S(Y)$ has order one there, $$\operatorname{ord}_t(x_j)=r^{j-1}$$ along the branch with zero residues. Suppose the first nonzero residue occurs at $x_i$, where $i\geq2$. It is an element $\alpha\in\mathbb F_r^*$, and $$\operatorname{ord}_t(x_i-\alpha)
 =\operatorname{ord}_t S(x_i)=r^{i-1}.$$ The numerator $x_i^{r+1}-\alpha^2$ is divisible by $x_i-\alpha$. Consequently, modulo integral series, $$\label{eq:pencil-first-shift}
 f(x_i)\equiv\frac{\alpha^2}{S(x_i)}
 =S\left(\frac{\alpha^2}{x_{i-1}}\right).$$ The equality uses $S(x_i)=f(x_{i-1})$, the last identity in Equation <a href="#eq:pencil-tower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:pencil-tower">[eq:pencil-tower]</a>, and $\alpha^2\in\mathbb F_r$. Thus this step splits after the specified shift, and every resulting root satisfies $x_{i+1}\equiv\alpha^2/x_{i-1}$ modulo integral series.

<a id="eq:pencil-descending-shifts"></a>
For any earlier small variable $x_j$, $j>1$, one has $$\label{eq:pencil-descending-shifts}
 \frac1{x_j}\equiv\frac1{S(x_j)}=S\left(\frac1{x_{j-1}}\right)
 \pmod{k[[t]]}.$$ Indeed the difference of the first two expressions is $x_j^{r-2}/(1+x_j^{r-1})$, which is integral. Since $f(Y)\equiv Y$ at a pole, Equations <a href="#eq:pencil-first-shift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:pencil-first-shift">[eq:pencil-first-shift]</a> and <a href="#eq:pencil-descending-shifts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:pencil-descending-shifts">[eq:pencil-descending-shifts]</a> give successively $$x_{i+s}\equiv\frac{\alpha^2}{x_{i-s}}\pmod{k[[t]]},
 \qquad 1\leq s\leq i-1,$$ for as long as those variables occur in the tower. Each step in this list is a splitting step with the indicated shift. The last principal part is $\alpha^2/x_1$, a simple pole. From then on every extension is the simple-pole extension of Lemma <a href="#lem:pencil-simple-pole" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pencil-simple-pole">3</a>. This description includes branches for which the tower stops before the first nonzero residue, during the descending shifts, or after ramification.

At every step the split factors or the single ramified factor account for the full relative degree $r$. Therefore, at each base place, the scalar extension of the degree-$N$ tower to $k((t))$ is exactly the product of the branch fields just constructed. No additional local factor is omitted.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Integral lattices and their determinants

Fix a base place with parameter $t$, and put $R=k[[t]]$. The product of its branch integral rings is a free $R$-module of rank $N$, by the bases constructed above. Express it in coefficient coordinates relative to Equation <a href="#eq:pencil-power-basis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:pencil-power-basis">[eq:pencil-power-basis]</a>, and denote the resulting lattice in $k((t))^N$ by $\Lambda$. If $C$ is a basis matrix of this lattice, define its coefficient determinant order by $\delta=\operatorname{ord}_t\det C$.

<a id="eq:pencil-norm-order"></a>
We use two elementary facts about this order. First, if a square matrix over $R$ has nonzero determinant, invertible row and column operations reduce it to a diagonal matrix. To see this, choose an entry of least order and move it to the first position. It divides every entry in $R$, so row and column subtractions clear its column and row. Continue in the remaining square matrix. The diagonal entries are units times powers of $t$. It follows that the dimension over $k$ of the cokernel equals the determinant order. Second, for a branch $k((z))$ over $k((t))$, multiplication by a unit has determinant a unit on $k[[z]]$, whereas multiplication by $z$ has cokernel $k$ and hence determinant order one. Thus $$\label{eq:pencil-norm-order}
 \operatorname{ord}_t\operatorname{Norm}_{k((z))/k((t))}(z^a u)=a
 \qquad(a\in\mathbb Z,\ u\in k[[z]]^*).$$ The equality for negative $a$ follows by multiplicativity. The residue field of each branch is $k$; this is why no residue-degree multiplier appears in Equation <a href="#eq:pencil-norm-order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:pencil-norm-order">[eq:pencil-norm-order]</a>.

<a id="eq:pencil-disc-tower"></a>
For completeness, if $b_1,\ldots,b_n$ is a basis of a separable algebra over a field and $c_1,\ldots,c_r$ is a relative basis for a separable algebra above it, their composed basis satisfies $$\label{eq:pencil-disc-tower}
 \operatorname{disc}(b_i c_j)
 =\operatorname{disc}(b_i)^r
   \operatorname{Norm}\bigl(\operatorname{disc}(c_j)\bigr).$$ Indeed, the evaluation map first applies $r$ copies of the lower evaluation matrix and then, over each lower embedding, its relative evaluation matrix. Taking determinants and squaring gives the formula. For a product algebra choose the relative bases componentwise; the same factorization applies.

The discriminant of the power basis in Equation <a href="#eq:pencil-power-basis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:pencil-power-basis">[eq:pencil-power-basis]</a> is a nonzero constant.
